{"file_path": "/workspace/LUMEN_Q1_V3.1/FORMAT_REFERENCE_E8_V3_1.md"}

### [20:54:28] RESULT
1	# Format CPU v3.1 — compatibilité binaire des fichiers valides
2	
3	12 novembre 2026. Les signatures binaires restent inchangées ; les gardes d’entrée sont renforcées.
4	
5	# Format CPU `LE8CPU3` / matrice `LMATv3` — spécification de référence
6	
7	**Ce format n’est pas Slot-CP-rANS, ni une implémentation CUDA de Lumen.** Son rôle : fournir des octets réels, un décodeur indépendant et des oracles de test. Voir `lumen_ref/entropy.py`.
8	
9	## 1. Codes et co-ensembles
10	
11	Le code stocké est `u=2z` pour `z∈E8`. Les huit coordonnées sont entières, de même parité et leur somme est divisible par4. La coquille est `m=Σu_i²/8` ; `m=0` possède un point, les autres `240·Σ_{d|m}d³`.
12	
13	Le rang suit un ordre déterministe : co-ensemble entier d’abord en mode0, puis demi-entier, puis ordre lexicographique des coordonnées admissibles. `e8.rank/unrank` réalise cette bijection par comptage dynamique. Il utilise de la mémoire cache CPU ; aucune affirmation de décodage GPU sans table n’est associée.
14	
15	## 2. En-tête de flux : 48 octets little-endian
16	
17	Structure Python `struct.Struct('<8sBBBBIQQQQ')` :
18	
19	| Champ | Taille |
20	|---|---:|
21	| magic `LE8CPU3\0` | 8 |
22	| mode :0uniforme,1conditionné par co-ensemble | 1 |
23	| limite de coquilles, défaut64, maximum128 | 1 |
24	| précision de fréquence, défaut14, intervalle1..16 | 1 |
25	| réservé=0 | 1 |
26	| taille groupe de rangs, défaut128, maximum4096 | 4 |
27	| nombre de vecteurs | 8 |
28	| longueur flux rANS | 8 |
29	| longueur flux de rangs | 8 |
30	| longueur échappements | 8 |
31	
32	Ensuite : table de fréquences entières `uint32`, flux rANS, flux de rangs, échappements, SHA-256 de32octets du corps complet précédent. La somme de contrôle détecte une corruption accidentelle ; elle n’authentifie pas un expéditeur.
33	
34	Mode0 : symboles0..limit−1 pour les coquilles, puis ESC. Mode1 : `2m+parité`, puis ESC=`2limit`. La contrainte couplée est `2^precision >= limit*(1+mode)+1`. Toutes les fréquences sont strictement positives ; le lissage et le coût des symboles impossibles éventuels sont donc visibles dans le modèle. L’arrondi déterministe des fréquences garantit une somme `2^precision`.
35	
36	Avec limite64, la table occupe260octets en mode0 ou516en mode1. Ce sont des tables **par flux**, pas une revendication globale3,5Kio sur27B.
37	
38	## 3. Flux rANS
39	
40	Une lane CPU, état initial de normalisation `L=2^23`. L’encodeur parcourt les symboles en sens inverse ; le décodeur les restitue dans l’ordre. État final stocké en `uint32 little-endian`, puis octets de renormalisation dans l’ordre de lecture. Le décodeur exige l’état terminal `L` et la consommation exacte de la section, même pour une liste vide.
41	
42	Un coût `−log2(f/2^precision)` est une longueur de modèle, **pas** la taille physique rANS. Le conteneur et l’état sont comptés séparément et réellement.
43	
44	## 4. Flux de rangs
45	
46	Pour chaque groupe de vecteurs non ESC, combiner les rangs `r_j` avec leurs radices `M_j` : `v←v·M_j+r_j`. Poser `P=∏M_j` ; le groupe occupe exactement `ceil(bit_length(P−1)/8)` octets big-endian, y compris les zéros de tête nécessaires. `P=1` n’émet aucun octet. Le décodeur obtient les radices des symboles de coquille déjà décodés, lit la longueur ainsi déterminée, puis inverse par un arbre de divisions euclidiennes équilibré. Le résultat binaire reste identique au schéma séquentiel ; le coût reste celui de grands entiers CPU.
47	
48	L’écart à `log2(P)` est inférieur à8bits par groupe. Ce résultat porte sur ce seul flux, pas sur tout le conteneur. Les grands entiers Python sont une réalisation de référence, pas une preuve de débit GPU.
49	
50	## 5. Échappements
51	
52	Une coquille hors limite émet ESC et huit int32 little-endian =32octets. Coût utilisé dans le modèle : coût du symbole ESC +256bits. Aucune saturation à la dernière coquille. L’encodeur refuse les coordonnées hors int32. Le décodeur valide l’appartenance à E8 et rejette un échappement non canonique qui aurait dû être dans les coquilles normales.
53	
54	## 6. Conteneur de matrice : 24 octets d’en-tête
55	
56	Structure `<8sIIQ` : magic `LMATv3\0\0`, lignes `m`, colonnes `n`, longueur du payload E8. Suivent `m` échelles FP16 little-endian, le payload `LE8CPU3`, puis un SHA-256 externe de32octets. `m` et `n` doivent être strictement positifs ; `n` doit être multiple de8. Le flux E8 seul accepte une liste vide, mais une matrice vide est refusée des deux côtés. Les poids reconstruits sont `W[i,j]=scale[i]·u[i,j]/2`.
57	
58	Les échelles sont arrondies à FP16 **avant le choix des codes et les évaluations**. `encode_matrix` exige ensuite que les valeurs passées soient exactement représentables dans ce format ; il n’effectue aucun arrondi silencieux supplémentaire. Zéros, NaN, infinis ou underflows non représentables sont refusés selon le contrat. Une ligne entièrement nulle reçoit une échelle1 et des codes0.
59	
60	Aucun Hadamard, D_in, profil d’état, codebook, facteur FP8 ni bias n’est implicitement ajouté à ce conteneur. L’appelant doit déclarer les coordonnées de la matrice ; les expériences fournies sont en base canonique. Toute conversion requise par un moteur complet doit être sérialisée et budgétée.
61	
62	## 7. Invariance de longueur à coquilles constantes
63	
64	À modèle de fréquence, mode, groupe, échelles et dimensions fixes, conserver position par position chaque coquille (et chaque co-ensemble en mode1), le statut ESC et l’ordre des vecteurs conserve les symboles rANS et les radices. L’état/flux rANS reste identique, les mots de rang gardent leur longueur, ESC garde32octets, les checksums gardent32octets. **La longueur totale est donc exactement conservée.**
65	
66	Les rangs et les poids changent ; la qualité peut empirer hors calibration. Cette propriété n’est pas vraie en général pour un autre codec dont les probabilités dépendent du voisinage ou pour des échelles réoptimisées.
67	
68	## 8. Périmètre non implémenté
69	
70	Pas de multi-lane CUDA, pas d’indexation aléatoire complète des embeddings, pas de packager18shards, pas de tables globales14profils, pas de compression des activations ni du cache. Limites de ressources des décodeurs à configurer pour un usage réel. Tests de corruption et de formes fournis, sans prétention de certification de sécurité exhaustive.
71	
72	## 9. Taille et limites pratiques
73	
74	Pour une matrice de `m` lignes, `N_f` fréquences et des sections de longueurs `l_a,l_b,l_e`, le fichier occupe exactement `136 + 2m + 4N_f + l_a + l_b + l_e` octets. Les tableaux et les en-têtes sont donc réellement inclus.
75	
76	Une permutation des vecteurs peut changer les frontières de groupes ou la longueur rANS, même si l’histogramme est identique. En mode 1, changer de co-ensemble sur une même coquille peut changer les radices et la taille. Les raffineurs conservent désormais le `group_size` de l’artefact, y compris s’il diffère de 128.
77	
78	Les tables sont par flux. L’efficacité de la version CPU n’est pas transposée automatiqueme

5	## 1. Ce qui change et ce qui ne change pas
6	
7	| Composant | Décision |
8	|---|---|
9	| Q0, références historiques L0/S0 | Conservées, immuables, distinctes du prototype CPU |

20	## 2. Passe 0 — Identité et contrats
21	
22	Conserver hashes des poids, tokenizer, processeur, template, runtime et kernels. Identifier les paramètres effectivement chargés, y compris MTP. Travailler sur copie et ne pas modifier Q0.
23	
24	Pour une transformation `W'=D_o^-1 W D_i U^T`, les entrées deviennent `x'=U D_i^-1 x`. Utiliser les statistiques dans ces mêmes coordonnées : `H'=U D_i^-1 H D_i^-1 U^T`, `G'=D_o^T G D_o`. La version CPU de matrice n’embarque pas automatiquement ces transformations : tout facteur nécessaire à l’inférence doit être sérialisé et compté.
25	
26	Un tenseur conservé en BF16 peut être accumulé en FP32 ; ce sont des objets différents du fichier. Toute modification continue doit être exportée dans le dtype réellement retenu avant évaluation. Le scaling défavorable de l’Étape 1 reste désactivé dans son régime, sans prétendre condamner tous les scalings.
27	
28	## 3. Passe 1 — Captures et statistiques
29	
30	Le schéma 2 valide des formes, des hashes et des partitions. Il n’atteste pas que le collecteur a exécuté le bon forward. Auditer séparément : mêmes préfixes et positions, normes complètes avant découpage, vraies entrées de la sous-couche, états et resets, biais Vision, chemin MTP et code exécuté.
31	
32	Ne pas utiliser les embeddings initiaux comme substituts aux entrées profondes. Pour un essai sur une tranche, conserver les autres contributions à la projection/non-linéarité ; sinon étiqueter le test comme système réduit.
33	
34	Séparer calibration, sélection et confirmation par documents/épisodes. Des colonnes `X_fp == X_q` peuvent être correctes en début de réseau ou avec préfixe intact. La diversité effective, le conditionnement et la régularisation comptent ; **aucun seuil universel `Ncal >= 10n` n’est adopté**.
35	
36	Les sondes Fisher ont une identité de covariance sous une distribution spécifiée. Les approximations bloc-diagonales/Kronecker ne deviennent pas le Hessien complet sans biais. Les statistiques de MTP et Vision exigent leurs propres entrées/sorties. Aucune nouvelle hiérarchie fixe des sensibilités n’est imposée.
37	
38	## 4. Passe 2 — Première expérience de calibration
39	
40	### Cible symétrique et cible asymétrique
41	
42	Comparer, sur les mêmes couples d’entrées :
43	
44	\[
45	J_{sym}(B)=\|B X_q-WX_q\|_F^2/N+\rho\|B-W\|_F^2,
46	\]
47	
48	\[
49	J_{asym}(B)=\|B X_q-WX_{fp}\|_F^2/N+\rho\|B-W\|_F^2.
50	\]
51	
52	Pour la seconde, les équations normales sont :
53	
54	\[
55	B(X_qX_q^T/N+\rho I)=W X_{fp}X_q^T/N+\rho W.
56	\]
57	
58	`asymmetric_target` résout cette cible continue. Ce n’est **pas** une reproduction de toutes les mises à jour séquentielles de GPTAQ. Elle doit être quantifiée, exportée, décodée et évaluée. Les biais et cibles observées sont pris en charge explicitement par l’appelant.
59	
60	Les candidats reçoivent soit un plafond commun avec une vraie frontière taille/qualité, soit une contrainte plus stricte `bytes(candidat) <= bytes(contrôle retenu)`. Inclure les paramètres du contrôle de départ dans sa grille. Le budget temps doit compter la résolution, les candidats, le codage et l’évaluation. Rapporter les coûts non égalisés.
61	
62	### Quantification et métriques
63	
64	`quantize_ldlq` est une référence lente en Python/NumPy. Les facteurs symétriques définis positifs sont contrôlés ; une matrice invalide ne reçoit pas de pivots artificiellement tronqués. Le sélecteur E8 recherche un ensemble fini de candidats : aucune optimalité globale du réseau n’est revendiquée.
65	
66	Les échelles sont arrondies avant la sélection. Les fichiers effectivement retournés sont décodés pour construire les poids évalués. Les coûts entropiques locaux servent à la recherche ; seul le fichier ferme le budget physique.
67	
68	## 5. Variante BGCC : expérimentale
69	
70	Avec porte quantifiée `g` et projection de sortie `D` fixées :
71	
72	\[
73	J(W)=\|D[g\odot(WX_q)]-Y_{fp}\|_F^2/N+\rho\|W-W_0\|_F^2.
74	\]
75	
76	L’opérateur normal appliqué à une variation `V` est :
77	
78	\[
79	\mathcal A(V)=\{g\odot[D^T D(g\odot VX_q)]\}X_q^T/N+\rho V.
80	\]
81	
82	La réalisation applique les produits sans construire tous les Grams par canal. Elle exige encore des activations et du calcul ; aucune durée H100 ni passage à l’échelle 27B n’est établi.
83	
84	**Condition de validation numérique :** le solveur doit retourner `converged=True` avec un vrai résidu vérifié. Sinon la cible n’est pas promue. Une résolution exacte du problème de calibration ne prouve pas un gain hors échantillon. Les résultats synthétiques antérieurs de BGCC restent irréguliers ; la variante est désactivée par défaut.
85	
86	Vision n’a pas nécessairement un SwiGLU. Un MLP `fc2(GELU(fc1(x)))` exige un autre problème de calibration ; on ne remplace pas SiLU par GELU dans une formule bilinéaire.
87	
88	## 6. Passe 3 — Récupération avec refus d’une régression
89	
90	Le polissage continu/discret peut être exploré seulement sous un protocole séparé. L’artefact accepté comprend codes, échelles, tables, groupements et métadonnées ; un rollback ne restaure pas un sous-ensemble de paramètres seulement.
91	
92	Une amélioration du critère à table fixe n’implique ni amélioration de sélection ni conservation des octets après refit. Vérifier les trois. L’invariance iso-coquille exige notamment mêmes symboles position par position, parité en mode 1, groupement, table et échelles. Les résultats négatifs historiques demeurent visibles.
93	
94	`compare_matrix_artifacts` exécute ce contrôle sur d

71	### Mécanismes documentés

### [] RESULT
1	# Format CPU v3.1 — compatibilité binaire des fichiers valides
2	
3	12 novembre 2026. Les signatures binaires restent inchangées ; les gardes d’entrée sont renforcées.
4	
5	# Format CPU `LE8CPU3` / matrice `LMATv3` — spécification de référence
6	
7	**Ce format n’est pas Slot-CP-rANS, ni une implémentation CUDA de Lumen.** Son rôle : fournir des octets réels, un décodeur indépendant et des oracles de test. Voir `lumen_ref/entropy.py`.
8	
9	## 1. Codes et co-ensembles
10	
11	Le code stocké est `u=2z` pour `z∈E8`. Les huit coordonnées sont entières, de même parité et leur somme est divisible par4. La coquille est `m=Σu_i²/8` ; `m=0` possède un point, les autres `240·Σ_{d|m}d³`.
12	
13	Le rang suit un ordre déterministe : co-ensemble entier d’abord en mode0, puis demi-entier, puis ordre lexicographique des coordonnées admissibles. `e8.rank/unrank` réalise cette bijection par comptage dynamique. Il utilise de la mémoire cache CPU ; aucune affirmation de décodage GPU sans table n’est associée.
14	
15	## 2. En-tête de flux : 48 octets little-endian
16	
17	Structure Python `struct.Struct('<8sBBBBIQQQQ')` :
18	
19	| Champ | Taille |
20	|---|---:|
21	| magic `LE8CPU3\0` | 8 |
22	| mode :0uniforme,1conditionné par co-ensemble | 1 |
23	| limite de coquilles, défaut64, maximum128 | 1 |
24	| précision de fréquence, défaut14, intervalle1..16 | 1 |
25	| réservé=0 | 1 |
26	| taille groupe de rangs, défaut128, maximum4096 | 4 |
27	| nombre de vecteurs | 8 |
28	| longueur flux rANS | 8 |
29	| longueur flux de rangs | 8 |
30	| longueur échappements | 8 |
31	
32	Ensuite : table de fréquences entières `uint32`, flux rANS, flux de rangs, échappements, SHA-256 de32octets du corps complet précédent. La somme de contrôle détecte une corruption accidentelle ; elle n’authentifie pas un expéditeur.
33	
34	Mode0 : symboles0..limit−1 pour les coquilles, puis ESC. Mode1 : `2m+parité`, puis ESC=`2limit`. La contrainte couplée est `2^precision >= limit*(1+mode)+1`. Toutes les fréquences sont strictement positives ; le lissage et le coût des symboles impossibles éventuels sont donc visibles dans le modèle. L’arrondi déterministe des fréquences garantit une somme `2^precision`.
35	
36	Avec limite64, la table occupe260octets en mode0 ou516en mode1. Ce sont des tables **par flux**, pas une revendication globale3,5Kio sur27B.
37	
38	## 3. Flux rANS
39	
40	Une lane CPU, état initial de normalisation `L=2^23`. L’encodeur parcourt les symboles en sens inverse ; le décodeur les restitue dans l’ordre. État final stocké en `uint32 little-endian`, puis octets de renormalisation dans l’ordre de lecture. Le décodeur exige l’état terminal `L` et la consommation exacte de la section, même pour une liste vide.
41	
42	Un coût `−log2(f/2^precision)` est une longueur de modèle, **pas** la taille physique rANS. Le conteneur et l’état sont comptés séparément et réellement.
43	
44	## 4. Flux de rangs
45	
46	Pour chaque groupe de vecteurs non ESC, combiner les rangs `r_j` avec leurs radices `M_j` : `v←v·M_j+r_j`. Poser `P=∏M_j` ; le groupe occupe exactement `ceil(bit_length(P−1)/8)` octets big-endian, y compris les zéros de tête nécessaires. `P=1` n’émet aucun octet. Le décodeur obtient les radices des symboles de coquille déjà décodés, lit la longueur ainsi déterminée, puis inverse par un arbre de divisions euclidiennes équilibré. Le résultat binaire reste identique au schéma séquentiel ; le coût reste celui de grands entiers CPU.
47	
48	L’écart à `log2(P)` est inférieur à8bits par groupe. Ce résultat porte sur ce seul flux, pas sur tout le conteneur. Les grands entiers Python sont une réalisation de référence, pas une preuve de débit GPU.
49	
50	## 5. Échappements
51	
52	Une coquille hors limite émet ESC et huit int32 little-endian =32octets. Coût utilisé dans le modèle : coût du symbole ESC +256bits. Aucune saturation à la dernière coquille. L’encodeur refuse les coordonnées hors int32. Le décodeur valide l’appartenance à E8 et rejette un échappement non canonique qui aurait dû être dans les coquilles normales.
53	
54	## 6. Conteneur de matrice : 24 octets d’en-tête
55	
56	Structure `<8sIIQ` : magic `LMATv3\0\0`, lignes `m`, colonnes `n`, longueur du payload E8. Suivent `m` échelles FP16 little-endian, le payload `LE8CPU3`, puis un SHA-256 externe de32octets. `m` et `n` doivent être strictement positifs ; `n` doit être multiple de8. Le flux E8 seul accepte une liste vide, mais une matrice vide est refusée des deux côtés. Les poids reconstruits sont `W[i,j]=scale[i]·u[i,j]/2`.
57	
58	Les échelles sont arrondies à FP16 **avant le choix des codes et les évaluations**. `encode_matrix` exige ensuite que les valeurs passées soient exactement représentables dans ce format ; il n’effectue aucun arrondi silencieux supplémentaire. Zéros, NaN, infinis ou underflows non représentables sont refusés selon le contrat. Une ligne entièrement nulle reçoit une échelle1 et des codes0.
59	
60	Aucun Hadamard, D_in, profil d’état, codebook, facteur FP8 ni bias n’est implicitement ajouté à ce conteneur. L’appelant doit déclarer les coordonnées de la matrice ; les expériences fournies sont en base canonique. Toute conversion requise par un moteur complet doit être sérialisée et budgétée.
61	
62	## 7. Invariance de longueur à coquilles constantes
63	
64	À modèle de fréquence, mode, groupe, échelles et dimensions fixes, conserver position par position chaque coquille (et chaque co-ensemble en mode1), le statut ESC et l’ordre des vecteurs conserve les symboles rANS et les radices. L’état/flux rANS reste identique, les mots de rang gardent leur longueur, ESC garde32octets, les checksums gardent32octets. **La longueur totale est donc exactement conservée.**
65	
66	Les rangs et les poids changent ; la qualité peut empirer hors calibration. Cette propriété n’est pas vraie en général pour un autre codec dont les probabilités dépendent du voisinage ou pour des échelles réoptimisées.
67	
68	## 8. Périmètre non implémenté
69	
70	Pas de multi-lane CUDA, pas d’indexation aléatoire complète des embeddings, pas de packager18shards, pas de tables globales14profils, pas de compression des activations ni du cache. Limites de ressources des décodeurs à configurer pour un usage réel. Tests de corruption et de formes fournis, sans prétention de certification de sécurité exhaustive.
71	
72	## 9. Taille et limites pratiques
73	
74	Pour une matrice de `m` lignes, `N_f` fréquences et des sections de longueurs `l_a,l_b,l_e`, le fichier occupe exactement `136 + 2m + 4N_f + l_a + l_b + l_e` octets. Les tableaux et les en-têtes sont donc réellement inclus.
75	
76	Une permutation des vecteurs peut changer les frontières de groupes ou la longueur rANS, même si l’histogramme est identique. En mode 1, changer de co-ensemble sur une même coquille peut changer les radices et la taille. Les raffineurs conservent désormais le `group_size` de l’artefact, y compris s’il diffère de 128.
77	
78	Les tables sont par flux. L’efficacité de la version CPU n’est pas transposée automatiqueme

202	# NOTE: the FLA package does not re-cast to `input_dtype` in its implementation, maybe we should do the same
203	@use_kernel_forward_from_hub("RMSNormGated")
204	class Qwen3_5RMSNormGated(nn.Module):
205	    def __init__(self, hidden_size: int, eps: float = 1e-6, **kwargs) -> None:
206	        super().__init__()
207	        self.weight = nn.Parameter(torch.ones(hidden_size))
208	        self.variance_epsilon = eps
209	        self.activation = "silu"
210	
211	    def forward(self, hidden_states: torch.Tensor, gate: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
212	        input_dtype = hidden_states.dtype
213	        hidden_states = hidden_states.to(torch.float32)
214	        variance = hidden_states.pow(2).mean(-1, keepdim=True)
215	        # Norm before gate
216	        hidden_states = hidden_states * torch.rsqrt(variance + self.variance_epsilon)
217	        hidden_states = self.weight * hidden_states.to(input_dtype)
218	        hidden_states = hidden_states * ACT2FN[self.activation](gate.to(torch.float32))
219	
220	        return hidden_states.to(input_dtype)
221	
222	
223	def apply_mask_to_padding_states(hidden_states, attention_mask):
224	    """
225	    Tunes out the hidden states for padding tokens, see https://github.com/state-spaces/mamba/issues/66
226	    """
227	    # NOTE: attention mask is a 2D boolean tensor
228	    if attention_mask is not None:
229	        dtype = hidden_states.dtype
230	        hidden_states = (hidden_states * attention_mask[:, :, None]).to(dtype)
231	
232	    return hidden_states
233	
234	
235	@use_kernel_func_from_hub_with_fallback("causal_conv1d_update", "causal_conv1d")
236	def causal_conv1d_update(
237	    hidden_states: torch.Tensor,
238	    conv_state: torch.Tensor,
239	    weight: nn.Parameter,
240	    bias: nn.Parameter | None = None,
241	    activation: str | None = None,
242	):
243	    _, hidden_size, seq_len = hidden_states.shape
244	    state_len = conv_state.shape[-1]
245	
246	    hidden_states_new = torch.cat([conv_state, hidden_states], dim=-1).to(weight.dtype)
247	    conv_state.copy_(hidden_states_new[:, :, -state_len:])
248	    out = F.conv1d(hidden_states_new, weight.unsqueeze(1), bias, padding=0, groups=hidden_size)
249	    out = out[:, :, -seq_len:]
250	    if activation is not None:
251	        out = ACT2FN[activation](out)
252	    return out.to(hidden_states.dtype)
253	
254	
255	@use_kernel_func_from_hub_with_fallback("causal_conv1d_fn", "causal_conv1d")
256	def causal_conv1d_fn(
257	    hidden_states: torch.Tensor,
258	    weight: nn.Parameter,
259	    bias: nn.Parameter | None = None,
260	    activation: str | None = None,
261	    **kwargs,
262	):
263	    _, hidden_size, seq_len = hidden_states.shape
264	    padding = weight.shape[-1] - 1
265	
266	    out = F.conv1d(
267	        hidden_states.to(weight.dtype),
268	        weight=weight.unsqueeze(1),
269	        bias=bias,
270	        padding=padding,
271	        groups=hidden_size,
272	    )[:, :, :seq_len]
273	    if activation is not None:
274	        out = ACT2FN[activation](out)
275	    return out.to(hidden_states.dtype)
276	
277	
278	# NOTE: the FLA package computes `x / torch.sqrt((x * x).sum(dim=dim, keepdim=True) + eps)` instead, so if we align
279	# with the GatedRMSNorm, maybe we can make that change as well.
280	def l2norm(x: torch.FloatTensor, dim: int = -1, eps: float = 1e-6):
281	    """This function is intended to align with the l2norm implementation in the FLA library."""
282	    inv_norm = torch.rsqrt((x * x).sum(dim=dim, keepdim=True) + eps)
283	    return x * inv_norm
284	
285	
286	@use_kernel_func_from_hub_with_fallback("chunk_gated_delta_rule", "fla")
287	def torch_chunk_gated_delta_rule(
288	    query: torch.Tensor,
289	    key: torch.Tensor,
290	    value: torch.Tensor,
291	    g: torch.Tensor,
292	    beta: torch.Tensor,
293	    chunk_size: int = 64,
294	    initial_state: torch.Tensor | None = None,
295	    output_final_state: bool = False,
296	    use_qk_l2norm_in_kernel: bool = False,
297	    **kwargs,
298	) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor | None]:
299	    """Computes the gated delta rule, by chunking along the sequence dimension.
300	    Args:
301	        query: Query tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_k_heads, k_head_dim]
302	        key: Key tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_k_heads, k_head_dim]
303	        value: Value tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_v_heads, v_head_dim]. num_v_heads can be equal
304	            to num_k_heads, same for v_head_dim and k_head_dim.
305	        g: Decay (in log space) tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_v_heads]: the recurrent state is
306	            multiplied by exp(g) at each step, so entries must be <= 0.
307	        beta: Beta tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_v_heads]
308	        chunk_size: Size of the chunks along the sequence dimension.
309	        initial_state: The recurrent state, an optional tensor of shape [batch_size, num_v_heads, k_head_dim, v_head_dim]
310	        output_final_state: Whether to output the new recurrent state along with the output.
311	        use_qk_l2norm_in_kernel: If this flag is set to True, query and key vectors are L2-normalized.
312	    Returns:
313	        - The output tensor of shape [batch_size, sequence_length, num_v_heads, v_head_dim]
314	        - Either None or the new recurrent state tensor of shape [batch_size, num_v_heads, k_head_dim, v_head_dim]
315	    """
316	    initial_dtype = query.dtype
317	    batch_size, sequence_length, _, k_head_dim = key.shape
318	    num_v_heads, v_head_dim = value.shape[-2:]
319	    recurrent_state_shape = (batch_size, num_v_heads, k_head_dim, v_head_dim)
320	    padded_output_shape = (batch_size, num_v_heads, -1, v_head_dim)  # -1 is the padded sequence length
321	    decay = g  # rename for clarity: argument name must stay "g" to match flash_linear_attention's API
322	
323	    # Make sure all tensors are fp32 and reshape them to [batch_size, num_*_heads, seqlen, ...]
324	    query, key, value, beta, decay = [
325	        x.transpose(1, 2).to(torch.float32, memory_format=torch.contiguous_format)
326	        for x in (query, key, value, beta, decay)
327	    ]
328	    # If enabled, normalize query and key vectors (in fp32 to match the FLA library)
329	    if use_qk_l2norm_in_kernel:
330	        query = l2norm(query, dim=-1, eps=1e-6)
331	        key = l2norm(key, dim=-1, eps=1e-6)
332	    # And always normalize queries by the head dimension
333	    scaling = query.shape[-1] ** -0.5
334	    query = query * scaling
335	
336	    # Pad sequence length to be a multiple of chunk_size. Padding is described as (left_pad, right_pad) for each dim.
337	    pad_size = (chunk_size - sequence_length % chunk_size) % chunk_size
338	    query, key, value = (F.pad(x, (0, 0, 0, pad_size)) for x in (query, key, value))
339	    beta, decay = (F.pad(x, (0, pad_size)) for x in (beta, decay))
340	
341	    total_sequence_length = sequence_length + pad_size
342

949	    def __init__(self, config) -> None:
950	        super().__init__()
951	        self.patch_size = config.patch_size
952	        self.temporal_patch_size = config.temporal_patch_size
953	        self.in_channels = config.in_channels
954	        self.embed_dim = config.hidden_size
955	
956	        kernel_size = [self.temporal_patch_size, self.patch_size, self.patch_size]
957	        self.proj = nn.Conv3d(self.in_channels, self.embed_dim, kernel_size=kernel_size, stride=kernel_size, bias=True)
958	
959	    def forward(self, hidden_states: torch.Tensor) -> torch.Tensor:
960	        target_dtype = self.proj.weight.dtype

963	        )

984	def apply_rotary_pos_emb_vision(
985	    q: torch.Tensor, k: torch.Tensor, cos: torch.Tensor, sin: torch.Tensor
986	) -> tuple[torch.Tensor, torch.Tensor]:
987	    orig_q_dtype = q.dtype
988	    orig_k_dtype = k.dtype
989	    q, k = q.float(), k.float()
990	    cos, sin = cos.unsqueeze(-2).float(), sin.unsqueeze(-2).float()
991	    q_embed = (q * cos) + (rotate_half(q) * sin)
992	    k_embed = (k * cos) + (rotate_half(k) * sin)
993	    q_embed = q_embed.to(orig_q_dtype)
994	    k_embed = k_embed.to(orig_k_dtype)
995	    return q_embed, k_embed

# Structural diagnostics on the BF16 weights (no model modification).
import json, math, time
H.restore()
lm = H.S["lm"]; out = {}
E = lm.embed_tokens.weight; Wh = model.lm_head.weight
def cgram(A, B=None, scale=None, chunk=16384):
    """sum over row chunks of A_c^T B_c in float64 (A, B [V, n])."""
    out = 0
    for i in range(0, A.shape[0], chunk):
        a = A[i:i+chunk].double(); b = a if B is None else B[i:i+chunk].double()
        if scale is not None: a = a * scale; b = a
        out = out + a.T @ b
    return out
# (1) is lm_head a linear function of embed_tokens?  min_M ||Wh - E M||
EtE = cgram(E); EtW = cgram(E, Wh)
M = torch.linalg.solve(EtE + 1e-8*EtE.diagonal().mean()*torch.eye(5120, device=E.device, dtype=torch.float64), EtW)
ss_res = 0.0; ss_tot = 0.0; cs = 0.0
for i in range(0, E.shape[0], 16384):
    e = E[i:i+16384].double(); w = Wh[i:i+16384].double()
    ss_res += float((w - e @ M).pow(2).sum()); ss_tot += float(w.pow(2).sum())
    cs += float(torch.nn.functional.cosine_similarity(e, w, dim=1).sum())
out["head_from_embed_R2"] = 1 - ss_res/ss_tot; out["cos_rowwise_mean"] = cs/E.shape[0]
print("lm_head ~ embed*M  R2=%.4f ; mean row cosine(embed, head)=%.4f" % (out["head_from_embed_R2"], out["cos_rowwise_mean"]))
del M, EtE, EtW, e, w
# (2) spectra: energy captured by top-r singular directions (plain and activation-weighted)
def spec_energy(W, X=None):
    if W.shape[0] > 100000:
        G = cgram(W, scale=None if X is None else X.double().sqrt().cuda()[None, :])
    else:
        W = W.double()
        if X is not None: W = W * X.double().sqrt().cuda()[None, :]
        G = W.T @ W if W.shape[0] >= W.shape[1] else W @ W.T
    ev = torch.linalg.eigvalsh(G).flip(0).clamp_min(0)
    cs = ev.cumsum(0)/ev.sum(); n = len(ev)
    p = ev/ev.sum(); erank = float(torch.exp(-(p[p>0]*p[p>0].log()).sum()))
    return {"n": n, "erank": erank, **{"top%d%%"%int(100*f): float(cs[int(n*f)-1]) for f in (0.125, 0.25, 0.5)}}
st = H.S["st"]
sp = {"embed": spec_energy(E), "lm_head": spec_energy(Wh), "lm_head_actw": spec_energy(Wh, st["X"]["lm_head"])}
for i in (1, 15, 31, 32, 47, 62):
    L = lm.layers[i]
    for k in ("gate_proj", "up_proj", "down_proj"):
        nm = f"layers.{i}.mlp.{k}"
        sp[nm] = spec_energy(getattr(L.mlp, k).weight); sp[nm+"_actw"] = spec_energy(getattr(L.mlp, k).weight, st["X"][nm])
    if hasattr(L, "linear_attn"):
        for k in ("in_proj_qkv", "in_proj_z", "out_proj"):
            nm = f"layers.{i}.linear_attn.{k}"; sp[nm] = spec_energy(getattr(L.linear_attn, k).weight); sp[nm+"_actw"] = spec_energy(getattr(L.linear_attn, k).weight, st["X"][nm])
    else:
        for k in ("q_proj", "o_proj"):
            nm = f"layers.{i}.self_attn.{k}"; sp[nm] = spec_energy(getattr(L.self_attn, k).weight); sp[nm+"_actw"] = spec_energy(getattr(L.self_attn, k).weight, st["X"][nm])
for k, v in sp.items(): print(k, {a: (round(b, 3) if isinstance(b, float) else b) for a, b in v.items()})
out["spectra"] = sp
# (3) weight statistics relevant to an E8 + per-row-step codec: kurtosis, row-RMS spread, massive input dims
ws = {}
for m in H.qmats():
    if m["layer"] not in (-1, 1, 31, 32, 62, 64): continue
    W = m["mod"].weight.float(); rms = W.pow(2).mean(1).sqrt()
    z = W / rms.clamp_min(1e-12)[:, None]
    d = dict(kurt_rownorm=float(z.pow(4).mean()), rowrms_cv=float(rms.std()/rms.mean()),
             colrms_cv=float(W.pow(2).mean(0).sqrt().std()/W.pow(2).mean(0).sqrt().mean()))
    if m["name"] in st["X"]:
        X = st["X"][m["name"]]; d["X_max_over_median"] = float(X.max()/X.median()); d["X_top8_share"] = float(X.topk(8).values.sum()/X.sum())
        G = st["G"][m["name"]]; d["G_p99_over_p01"] = float(torch.quantile(G[:100000], .99)/torch.quantile(G[:100000], .01).clamp_min(1e-30))
    ws[m["name"]] = d; print(m["name"], {a: round(b, 3) for a, b in d.items()})
out["weight_stats"] = ws
# (4) SwiGLU channel saliency concentration (Taylor): share of saliency in the weakest channels
ts = {}
for i in range(64):
    s = st["TS"][f"layers.{i}.mlp.down_proj"].double().sort().values; c = s.cumsum(0)/s.sum()
    ts[i] = [float(c[int(len(s)*f)-1]) for f in (0.125, 0.25, 0.5)]
print("Taylor saliency share of weakest 12.5/25/50% channels, layers 0,8,..:", {i: [round(a, 4) for a in ts[i]] for i in range(0, 64, 8)})
out["taylor_share_weakest"] = ts
json.dump(out, open(H.OUT+"/diag_structure.json", "w"))

# Structure of what remains AFTER the real E8-LDLQ (BF16 prefix, real inputs), to decide three families of ideas:
#  (a) low-rank protection / correction (QERA, SRR): share of the activation-weighted error in its top-r directions,
#      against the same bytes spent on E8 precision;
#  (b) exceptions / channel splitting (SpQR, OCS): concentration of the error on few entries / columns;
#  (c) factorise-then-quantise: truncation loss (activation metric) of the rank affordable at equal bytes.
import importlib, seq, json, math, time
import formic_q as fq

H.restore(); lm = H.S["lm"]; out = {}; mult = H.S["mult_Q0ldlq"]
sq = SQ.Seq(n_docs=144); want = {1, 16, 31, 32, 47, 62}
def top_share(ev, rs=(16, 64, 256)):
    ev = ev.clamp_min(0).flip(0); tot = ev.sum()
    return {r: float(ev[:r].sum() / tot) for r in rs}
for i, L in enumerate(lm.layers):
    if i in want:
        gd = hasattr(L, "linear_attn")
        todo = [("mixer_in", (L.linear_attn.in_proj_qkv if gd else L.self_attn.q_proj)), ("mixer_out", (L.linear_attn.out_proj if gd else L.self_attn.o_proj)),
                ("mlp_gate", L.mlp.gate_proj), ("mlp_down", L.mlp.down_proj)]
        for nm, mod in todo:
            Hm = sq.gram(i, mod); W = mod.weight.float(); m_, n_ = W.shape
            r_ = fq.row_steps(W, mult)
            Q, hist, _ = fq.quantize_ldlq(W, Hm.clone(), r_, None, damp=0.01)
            E = (W - Q).double(); Wd = W.double()
            sig = float(((Wd @ Hm) * Wd).sum()); err = float(((E @ Hm) * E).sum())
            rec = dict(shape=[m_, n_], bpw=fq.matrix_bits(hist, m_, n_) / (m_ * n_), rel_proxy_err=err / sig, rel_frob_err=float(E.norm() / Wd.norm()))
            # (a) spectrum of the activation-weighted error and signal on the smaller side
            if m_ <= n_:
                ev_e = torch.linalg.eigvalsh((E @ Hm) @ E.T); ev_w = torch.linalg.eigvalsh((Wd @ Hm) @ Wd.T)
            else:
                lam, V = torch.linalg.eigh(Hm); Hh = (V * lam.clamp_min(0).sqrt()) @ V.T; del V
                A = E @ Hh; ev_e = torch.linalg.eigvalsh(A.T @ A); A = Wd @ Hh; ev_w = torch.linalg.eigvalsh(A.T @ A); del A, Hh
            rec["err_top_share"] = top_share(ev_e); rec["sig_top_share"] = top_share(ev_w)
            # same bytes in E8 precision: rank r FP16 factors cost 16 r (m+n) bits -> db bits/weight -> error x 4^-db
            rec["lowrank_vs_bits"] = {}

# Why did LDL-pivot column scaling not help in KL?  Local check at matched bits (BF16 prefix, real inputs):
# activation-weighted output error of E8-LDLQ with the reference steps vs with column scales (clip 4 and 2).
import importlib, seq, json
import formic_q as fq
importlib.reload(seq); SQ = seq
H.restore(); lm = H.S["lm"]; out = {}
sq = SQ.Seq(n_docs=144); want = {3, 16, 31, 47, 62}
def run(W, Hm, c, target_bpw):
    m_, n_ = W.shape; lo, hi = 0.2, 2.5; Wc = W if c is None else W * c
    for _ in range(9):                                  # bisection on the real LDLQ bits of this matrix
        mid = (lo * hi) ** 0.5; r = fq.row_steps(Wc, mid)
        Q, hist, _ = fq.quantize_ldlq(W, Hm.clone(), r, c, damp=0.01)
        bpw = fq.matrix_bits(hist, m_, n_, has_col_scale=c is not None) / (m_ * n_)
        if bpw > target_bpw: lo = mid
        else: hi = mid
    E = (W - Q).double(); Wd = W.double()
    return bpw, float(((E @ Hm) * E).sum() / ((Wd @ Hm) * Wd).sum()), float(hist[-1] / hist.sum())
for i, L in enumerate(lm.layers):
    if i in want:
        gd = hasattr(L, "linear_attn")
        for nm, mod in (("mixer_in", (L.linear_attn.in_proj_z if gd else L.self_attn.k_proj)), ("mixer_out", (L.linear_attn.out_proj if gd else L.self_attn.o_proj)), ("mlp_down", L.mlp.down_proj)):
            Hm = sq.gram(i, mod); W = mod.weight.float()
            if nm == "mlp_down": W = W[:1024]          # rows are independent: a row subset is enough for the proxy
            if W.shape[0] > 2048: W = W[:2048]
            res = {"lumen": run(W, Hm, None, 2.82)}
            for clip in (2.0, 4.0, 64.0):
                c = H._ldlcol(Hm, dict(col_clip=clip)); res[f"ldlcol_clip{clip:g}"] = run(W, Hm, c, 2.82)
            out[f"L{i}.{nm}"] = res
            print("L%-2d %-9s" % (i, nm), " | ".join("%s: bpw %.3f err %.5f esc %.1e" % (k, *v) for k, v in res.items()), flush=True)
            del Hm
    sq.advance(i)
    if i >= max(want): break
json.dump(out, open(H.OUT + "/diag_ldlcol_proxy.json", "w"), indent=1); del sq

The paper is arXiv:2402.17762, "Massive Activations in Large Language Models," by Mingjie Sun, Xinlei Chen, J. Zico Kolter, and Zhuang Liu. It was submitted on 27 Feb 2024 and appeared at COLM 2024.

The results support the core of your description but not the "set to mean" detail:

- **Constant bias behavior:** The authors find that the values of these activations stay largely fixed across inputs, so they act as bias terms. Second, we find their values largely stay constant regardless of the input, and they function as indispensable bias terms in LLMs.
- **Scale and location:** In LLaMA2-7B, setting merely four massive activations (out of millions of activations) to zero would result in catastrophic collapse in model performance.
- **Fix via explicit biases:** Furthermore, we find that explicit attention biases eliminate the need of LLMs to learn massive activations during pretraining.

I did not find any mention of setting the constant bias terms to their mean. The search results only describe the bias role, the zeroing experiment, and the explicit-bias remedy. If that detail matters to you, check the full paper at arxiv.org/abs/2402.17762 or the code at github.com/locuslab/massive-activations.

The search confirmed the paper and its main results, but none of the results give the E8 figure of 0.0717.

- **The paper:** Zamir and Feder's "On lattice quantization noise" appeared in IEEE Transactions on Information Theory in 1996 (vol. 42, no. 4, pp. 1152–1159). For the optimal lattice quantizers this noise is wide-sense-stationary and white.
- **Entropy-coded dithered quantization:** For large lattice dimension, both the error and the bit rate approach the error and the information rate of an additive white Gaussian noise channel.
- **Normalized second moment:** One indexed abstract snippet says that as dimension grows, the normalized second moment of the optimal lattice quantizer tends to 1/(2πe) (about 0.0585), which is the origin of the limit that E8 is compared against. This is from the snippet, not from the search itself.
- **E8 value:** None of the results state the E8 normalized second moment of 0.0717. That value is commonly cited in the literature, but I could not verify it from these sources. A later paper by Agrell and Allen, "On the Best Lattice Quantizers" (IEEE Trans. Inf. Theory, 2023), discusses normalized second moments of optimal lattices in various dimensions, but the snippets don't include E8's figure.

To confirm 0.0717, check Conway and Sloane's *Sphere Packings, Lattices and Groups* or the Zamir–Feder paper's tables directly.

The search didn't return the arXiv 2211.10438 paper itself, but several secondary sources confirm the formula and the α = 0.5 default.

- **Formula:** A 2024 Mamba-PTQ paper gives the per-channel scale as s_j = max(|X_j|)^α / max(|W_j|)^(1−α) and describes α as controlling how much quantization difficulty moves from activations to weights. It introduces a per-channel smoothing factor s, where C_i is the number of activation dimensions and output channels of the weight matrix. The Intel Neural Compressor docs use the same expression, and note that a larger α suits models with more significant activation outliers.
- **Default α = 0.5:** The Mamba-PTQ paper says the authors suggest a default of α=0.5, with larger values for models where activation outliers are more significant. A 2024 low-bit LLM survey adds that α = 0.5 is a well-balanced point for all OPT and BLOOM models, though the survey notes that other models may require multiple trials to find the best value.
- **Practical tuning:** Intel Neural Compressor's docs describe two ways to apply smooth quantization: using a fixed alpha for the entire model, or determining alpha through auto-tuning.

I couldn't confirm the paper's exact wording from the arXiv abstract, so the α = 0.5 default is best verified against the original PDF.

The search found the paper (arXiv 2402.04396, Tseng, Chee, Sun, Kuleshov, De Sa, ICML 2024) and confirmed its main components. It did not surface the incoherence theorem or the proxy loss bound themselves, so I can't confirm those details from these results.

- **Paper identity:** The arXiv listing shows the paper was submitted on 6 Feb 2024 (v1), last revised 4 Jun 2024 (this version, v2) under the title "QuIP#: Even Better LLM Quantization with Hadamard Incoherence and Lattice Codebooks."
- **BlockLDLQ and E8:** The paper uses the BlockLDLQ block adaptive rounding algorithm with compressible codebooks based on the E8 lattice. One summary describes BlockLDLQ as an extension of the LDLQ adaptive rounding algorithm that supports vector quantization.
- **Codebook:** The E8P codebook is described as a 2-bit codebook with 216 entries, but it is structured such that lookups only need to be performed on a 28-entry table. This comes from a secondary review, so check it against the paper.
- **Incoherence processing:** The paper uses the computationally-efficient randomized Hadamard transform. A Hugging Face summary says this version has better theoretical properties than the Kronecker approach in QuIP.
- **LDLQ optimality:** An excerpt of the paper says LDLQ is optimal within the class of adaptive rounding methods with linear feedback and offers provably better error rates than nearest or stochastic rounding. This is cited to the earlier QuIP work.

For the theorem and proxy loss bound, the next step is to open the PDF or HTML version of arXiv 2402.04396 directly and read the incoherence section.

Web search results for query: "SliceGPT computational invariance orthogonal transformation RMSNorm LayerNorm to RMSNorm fold mean subtraction arXiv 2401.15024"

Links: [{"title":"SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.15024"},{"title":"arxiv.org","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2401.15024v2.pdf"},{"title":"[Revue de papier] SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.themoonlight.io/fr/review/slicegpt-compress-large-language-models-by-deleting-rows-and-columns"},{"title":"[Papierüberprüfung] SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.themoonlight.io/de/review/slicegpt-compress-large-language-models-by-deleting-rows-and-columns"},{"title":"[Literature Review] SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.themoonlight.io/review/slicegpt-compress-large-language-models-by-deleting-rows-and-columns"},{"title":"[Revisión de artículo] SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.themoonlight.io/es/review/slicegpt-compress-large-language-models-by-deleting-rows-and-columns"},{"title":"[論文評述] SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.themoonlight.io/tw/review/slicegpt-compress-large-language-models-by-deleting-rows-and-columns"},{"title":"SliceGPT: Compress Large Language Models by Deleting Rows and Columns","url":"https://www.arxiv-vanity.com/papers/2401.15024"},{"title":"www.emergentmind.com","url":"https://www.emergentmind.com/topics/slicegpt"},{"title":"arxiv.org","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/2506.02818v1"}]

The search turned up the paper itself plus several secondary summaries, and they agree on the core mechanism.

- **Invariance:** The paper says an orthogonal matrix Q can be inserted before RMSNorm and its transpose after it, leaving the network's output unchanged. RMSNorm(XQ)Q^T = RMSNorm(X) is the key identity (Equation 2, with a proof in Appendix A.1). Because of this, the weights can be transformed by any orthogonal Q and the computation still matches the original.
- **LayerNorm conversion:** LayerNorm is written as an RMSNorm applied after a mean-removal matrix M, followed by a diagonal scale and an offset. The mean-removal matrix is the constant I − (1/D)·11^T, which removes the row mean. The scale (α) gets absorbed into adjacent weights, and the offset (β) is handled separately. Secondary summaries describe this as folding the scale into neighboring matrices and moving the mean subtraction into the output weights. LayerNorm(X) = RMSNorm(XM)diag(α)√D + 1Nβ⊤ is the formula for this, per the paper's Equation 1.
- **Slicing:** Once the network is in the rotated basis, the PCA-based directions with the smallest eigenvalues are removed by deleting the matching rows or columns of the weight matrices. Summaries note that each block gets its own rotation Q_ℓ, computed from the eigenvectors of a calibration covariance matrix.

I didn't fetch the full paper, so the exact placement of the mean-subtraction fold and the β handling come from the paper's Section 3 excerpt and secondary summaries; check Section 3 and Appendix A of arXiv 2401.15024 for the precise details.

Web search results for query: "Nagel "Data-Free Quantization Through Weight Equalization and Bias Correction" cross-layer equalization scaling equivariance arXiv 1906.04721"

Links: [{"title":"Data-Free Quantization Through Weight Equalization and Bias Correction","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/1906.04721"},{"title":"Data-Free Quantization","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/1906.04721.pdf"},{"title":"arxiv.org","url":"https://arxiv.org/pdf/1906.04721v1.pdf"},{"title":"arxiv.org","url":"https://arxiv.org/abs/1906.04721v3"},{"title":"Data-Free Quantization","url":"https://www.arxiv-vanity.com/papers/1906.04721/"},{"title":"web3.arxiv.org","url":"https://web3.arxiv.org/pdf/1906.04721v1"},{"title":"Data-Free Quantization","url":"https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/1906.04721"},{"title":"www.emergentmind.com","url":"https://www.emergentmind.com/papers/1906.04721"},{"title":"www.emergentmind.com","url":"https://www.emergentmind.com/articles/1906.04721"},{"title":"Data-Free Quantization through Weight Equalization and Bias Correction","url":"https://deepai.org/publication/data-free-quantization-through-weight-equalization-and-bias-correction"}]

The paper is Markus Nagel, Mart van Baalen, Tijmen Blankevoort, and Max Welling's "Data-Free Quantization Through Weight Equalization and Bias Correction" (arXiv:1906.04721). It was published at ICCV 2019 and its latest arXiv version is v3, revised 25 Nov 2019.

- **Core idea:** The method rescales weights so they are easier to quantize, using the scale-equivariance of activation functions, and then corrects the bias that quantization adds. Their approach relies on equalizing the weight ranges in the network by making use of a scale-equivariance property of activation functions.
- **Scaling equivariance:** Per one summary, the technique exploits positive scaling equivariance by adjusting weights so they suit quantization (By adjusting the weights so that they are more amenable to quantization, the technique exploits positive scaling equivariance.). The search results don't include the actual cross-layer equalization equations, so I can't confirm the exact formulation from them.
- **Bias correction:** The quantization process introduces biased error on the outputs, with non-trivial effects on subsequent network layers. The paper's correction is analytical and uses batch normalization parameters, per one summary (it uses batch normalization parameters for analytical bias correction).
- **Data-free:** The method needs no data, fine-tuning, or hyperparameter selection. It achieves near-original model performance on common computer vision architectures and tasks.
- **Prior related work:** The paper notes that [22] also exploits the scale equivariance of the ReLU function to rescale weight channels and notice the biased error introduced by weight quantization [7], leading to a method that resembles our data-free quantization approach. The authors say they developed their work independently of it.

For the exact equalization and bias-correction derivations, the arXiv PDF (arxiv.org/pdf/1906.04721) is the primary source.

  b. Optimal per-channel scale: Section 4.1.2, Eq. (11):
     s_i = (1 / r_i^(2)) * sqrt(r_i^(1) r_i^(2))
     Objective, stated in the verbatim text before Eq. (9): "We want to find S such that the total precision per channel is maximized:" Eq. (9): max_S sum_i p_hat_i^(1) p_hat_i^(2). Precision is defined in Eq. (8) as p_hat_i^(1) = r_hat_i^(1) / R_hat^(1).
     Necessary condition, Eq. (10): argmax_j (1/s_j) r_j^(1) = argmax_k s_k r_k^(2). Eq. (11) "results in ∀i: r_i^(1) = r_i^(2)".
     Caveat: the paper's own text does not say Eq. (11) "maximizes" anything directly; it says it satisfies the condition from Eq. (10) and matches the ranges. The appendix derivation (Eqs. 20-28) is only in the model summary, which I could not verify verbatim, so do not cite those numbers as checked.
  
  c. Version and venue: The ar5iv page shows no v1/v2/v3 label and no venue. Unknown from this page.
  
  **2) Xiao et al., "SmoothQuant"**
  URL read: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2211.10438
  
  a. Smoothing factor, Section 4, Eq. (4):
     s_j = max(|X_j|)^alpha / max(|W_j|)^(1-alpha)
     Migration strength: "Here we introduce a hyper-parameter, migration strength α, to control how much difficulty we want to migrate from activation to weights, using the following equation:" Eq. (3) is the equivalence Y = (X diag(s)^-1)(diag(s) W).
  
  b. Recommended alpha, Section 4 (verbatim): "We find that for most of the models, e.g., all OPT ... and BLOOM ... models, α = 0.5 is a well-balanced point to evenly split the quantization difficulty". For GLM-130B: "we can choose a larger α to migrate more quantization difficulty to weights (like 0.75)." Section 5.1 says α = 0.5 for OPT/BLOOM and α = 0.75 for GLM-130B. Table 6 (LLaMA) uses α = 0.8. Table 7 gives per-model α: Llama-2-7B 0.85, Llama-2-13B 0.85, Llama-2-70B 0.9, Falcon-7B 0.6, Falcon-40B 0.7, Mistral-7B 0.8, Mixtral-8x7B 0.8.
     alpha=0 and alpha=1: the paper does not label these. By substituting into Eq. (4), alpha=1 gives s_j = max|X_j| and alpha=0 gives s_j = 1/max|W_j|. Section 4 says the first "pushes all the quantization difficulties to the weights" and the second makes "the model performance is bad due to the activation quantization errors." These are my mapping of the paper's text, not its own statement.
     Caveat: the summary says the sweet spot is 0.4-0.6, and that alpha below 0.4 or above 0.6 is bad. I did not see this in verbatim text, so treat it as unverified.
  
  c. Version and venue: The page shows no arXiv version. The keywords line reads "Machine Learning, ICML", so the venue is ICML according to that page.
  
  **3) Lin et al., "AWQ: Activation-aware Weight Quantization"**
  URL read: https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2306.00978
  
  a. Search, Section 3.2:
     Eq. (4): s* = argmin_s L(s), with L(s) = || Q(W · diag(s)) (diag(s)^-1 · X) − W X ||.
     Eq. (5): s = s_X^alpha, alpha* = argmin_alpha L(s_X^alpha). Here s_X is the per-channel average activation magnitude.
     Grid: "We can find the best α by a fast grid search over the interval of [0,1]" (0 means no scaling, 1 is the most aggressive). Section 5.1: "We used a grid size of 20 to search for the optimal α." The paper also applies weight clipping to minimize quantization MSE.
  
  b. Delta'/Delta argument, Section 3.2. Quantizer Eq. (1): Q(w) = Δ · Round(w/Δ), Δ = max(|w|)/2^(N-1).
     Eq. (2): Q(w·s)·(x/s) = Δ' · Round(w s / Δ') · x · (1/s).
     Eq. (3): Err(Q(w)x) = Δ · RoundErr(w/Δ) · x, and Err(Q(w·s)(x/s)) = Δ' · RoundErr(w s/Δ') · x · (1/s).
     Ratio stated in the text: "The ratio of the new error to the original error is Δ′/Δ · 1/s." Assumptions: RoundErr ≈ 0.25 unchanged, and Δ' ≈ Δ. "Given Δ′≈Δ and s>1, the relative error is smaller for the salient weight w."
     Table 2 (OPT-6.7B): average Δ'/Δ is 1.005, 1.013, 1.038, 1.213 for s = 1.25, 1.5, 2, 4. Wiki-2 PPL is best at s=2 (11.92). The paper also notes the error for non-salient channels grows when Δ' > Δ.
  
  c. Venue and version: "Proceedings of the 7th MLSys Conference, Santa Clara, CA, USA, 2024. Best Paper Award." The page shows no arXiv version number.
  
  Note: ar5iv renders one version of each paper. None of the three pages states which arXiv version it is.

  **1. Massive Activations in Large Language Models (Sun, Chen, Kolter, Liu)**
  URL read: https://arxiv.org/html/2402.17762v2 (header: "arXiv:2402.17762v2 [cs.CL] 14 Aug 2024"). I did not check v1.
  Venue: the abstract footnote says "Published as a conference paper in First Conference on Language Modeling (COLM), 2024."
  
  a. Definition (Section 2, "Massive Activations", Table 1): the paper gives a "loose but broad definition". An activation counts as massive if "its magnitude surpasses 100" and is "at least or around 1,000 times larger than the median magnitude of its hidden state." Table 1 gives LLaMA2-7B top-1 2622.0, top-2 1547.0, median 0.2. The intro says these are "more than 4 orders of magnitude larger than the median." The 1,000x figure is a rule of thumb, not a hard threshold.
  
  b. Intervention (Section 3, Table 3, LLaMA2-7B; the same experiment on LLaMA2-13B is in the same table). The intervention is applied at layer 2 for LLaMA2-7B and layer 4 for LLaMA2-13B.
  - LLaMA2-7B, Original: WikiText 5.47, C4 7.85, PG-19 8.57, zero-shot 68.95%.
  - LLaMA2-7B, Set to zero: inf, inf, inf, 36.75%.
  - LLaMA2-7B, Set to mean: 5.47, 7.86, 8.59, 68.94%.
  - LLaMA2-13B, Original: 4.88, 7.22, 7.16, 71.94%. Set to zero: 5729, 5526, 4759, 37.50%. Set to mean: 4.88, 7.22, 7.16, 71.92%.
  The text says zeroing gives "exploding perplexity numbers." Zeroing ordinary activations of similar magnitude to the median "leads to no performance drop."
  
  c. Normalization, residual norm, bias terms.
  - The paper says "massive activations act as fixed but important biases in LLMs" (end of Section 3). The abstract says they "function as indispensable bias terms in LLMs."
  - Section 4.2 mentions the normalization step. "We hypothesize that the attention LayerNorm may play a pivotal role in this process." Footnote 3 says LLaMA2 uses RMSNorm.
  - I did not verify any residual-stream-norm claim in the verbatim text.
  - LLaMA2-7B: feature dimensions 1415 and 2533 (Section 2.2, Figure 1). They first appear at layer 2 and stay "nearly constant values until layer 30" (Section 2.1).
  
  d. Tokens (Section 2.2, verbatim): "the starting word token" and "the token representing the first period (.) or newline token (\n) in the sequence." Section 2 summary: "Starting token and the first 'strong' delimiter token." Section 3 "Why these layers and tokens?" gives the reasoning that the start token occurs in every training sequence.
  
  e. Venue: COLM 2024 (see above).
  
  **2. Understanding and Minimising Outlier Features in Neural Network Training (He, Noci, Paliotta, Schlag, Hofmann)**
  URL read: https://arxiv.org/abs/2405.19279. Title and authors match your query exactly. The HTML body was not read.
  a. Abstract, verbatim:
  - "Outlier Features (OFs) are neurons whose activation magnitudes significantly exceed the average over a neural network's (NN) width."
  - "little is known behind why OFs emerge during training, nor how one can minimise them."
  - It uses metrics such as "the kurtosis over neuron activation norms" and studies "how architectural and optimisation choices influence OFs."
  - It names a "default OF-prone combination of Pre-Norm model and Adam."
  - It introduces "the Outlier Protected block" (an unnormalised transformer block) and "a previously unknown benefit of non-diagonal preconditioning optimisers."
  The abstract does not explicitly state that normalisation layers, Adam, or learning rate cause OFs. The causal framing is in the body, which I did not read.
  b. arXiv v2, submitted 29 May 2024 (v1), revised 6 Nov 2024 (v2). Comment: "NeurIPS 2024 camera ready." Venue: NeurIPS 2024. Licence CC BY 4.0.
  
  **3. Privileged Bases in the Transformer Residual Stream (Elhage, Lasenby, Olah)**
  URL read: https://transformer-circuits.pub/2023/privileged-basis/index.html
  a. Main claim and cause (verbatim abstract):
  - "individual coordinates in the residual stream should have no special significance"
  - "we provisionally conclude that the per-dimension normalizers in the Adam optimizer are to blame for the effect."
  - "We confidently rule these out as being the source of the observed basis-alignment." (Layer normalization and floating-point precision were the two alternatives tested.)
  Outlier definition (verbatim): an "outlier" is "a single scalar value in the residual stream whose absolute value is >6."
  Measurement: the abstract does not name a measure. The "privileged basis" test and kurtosis were not in the verbatim text I read. The model summary said they use kurtosis, with 3 as the Gaussian reference, and mentioned "20–60 outlier dimensions" out of d_model=1280. I did not verify these numbers, so treat them as unconfirmed.
  b. Published March 16, 2023. Authors: Nelson Elhage, Robert Lasenby, Christopher Olah. Affiliation: Anthropic.

  Structured report. Quotes are short; formulas are as rendered on the pages.
  
  PAGES READ
  - https://ar5iv.labs.arxiv.org/html/2307.13304 (ar5iv render; text cut at ~40k of 65k chars, the rest came only as a model summary, so sections after Sec. 5.2 are not verified)
  - https://arxiv.org/abs/2307.13304 (metadata)
  - https://arxiv.org/html/2402.04396v2 (read in two offset chunks, 0-39956 and 39956-79912; the rest of the paper, including Sec. 4.3 and Appendix B, was not read verbatim)
  
  1) QuIP (Chee, Cai, Kuleshov, De Sa), arXiv 2307.13304
  
  a. Definitions
  - Proxy objective, Eq. (1): ℓ(Ŵ) = E_x[‖(Ŵ−W)x‖²] = tr((Ŵ−W)H(Ŵ−W)^T). (Sec. 3, intro to Sec. 3.1). H is the second moment of calibration inputs, the "proxy Hessian".
  - LDLQ update, Sec. 3.1 (unnumbered display): Ŵ_k = Q(W_k + (W_{1:(k−1)} − Ŵ_{1:(k−1)}) a_k), with Q nearest or stochastic rounding.
  - Matrix form, Eq. (2): Ŵ = Q(W + (W − Ŵ)U), U strictly upper triangular, Q acting elementwise.
  - Eq. (3): tr((Ŵ−W)H(Ŵ−W)^T) = tr(η(U+I)^{-1} H (U+I)^{-T} η^T), with η the quantization error.
  - LDL choice, Eq. (4): H = (Ǔ+I) D (Ǔ+I)^T, D non-negative diagonal, Ǔ upper unit triangular. Setting U ← Ǔ makes the (U+I) factors cancel. (Note: Algorithm 3, line 3 prints "(Ǔ+I)D(Ǔ+I)^{-1}", which looks like a typo for the transpose.)
  
  b. Theorem 1 (Sec. 3.2). Quoted: "LDLQ is worst and average-case optimal amongst rounding methods which specify the linear feedback U as a function of H (not of W), and when rounding to the integers."
  Formula: (m/4) tr(D) = L_worst(LDLQ, H) ≤ L_worst(A, H), and (m/c) tr(D) = L_avg(LDLQ, H) ≤ L_avg(A, H), with c = 12 for nearest and c = 6 for stochastic rounding.
  Worst/average losses are defined in Eqs. (5) and (6).
  Lemma 3 (Sec. 3.3) gives the baselines: L_worst(Stoch, H) = (m/4) tr(H), and L_avg({Near, Stoch}, H) = (m/c) tr(H).
  
  c. Lemma 2 (Sec. 3.3). Assumes H is μ-incoherent (Definition 1: |Q_ij| ≤ μ/√n for H = QΛQ^T). Statement: tr(D) ≤ (μ²/n) tr(H^{1/2})².
  Comparison: tr(D) < tr(H̃) for non-diagonal H̃ ⪰ 0 (Sec. 3.2). Combining Lemma 2 with Theorem 1 and Lemma 3 gives, for rank-k H with μ²k < n: L_worst(LDLQ, H) ≤ (mμ²/4n) tr(H^{1/2})² ≤ (mμ²k/4n) tr(H) ≤ (m/4) tr(H). This is the chain in Sec. 3.3, unnumbered display.
  Theorem 4 (Sec. 3.3) says that without incoherence, no spectral bound separates LDLQ from the baselines.
  
  d. Theorem 6 (Sec. 5.1): "OTPQ [sic] falls within the class of adaptive rounding procedures with linear feedback ... and is equivalent to LDLQ in Section 3." (Typo "OTPQ" is in the paper.)
  
  e. Version and venue
  - arXiv listing: v1 submitted 25 Jul 2023; v2 revised 15 Jan 2024 (the "this version"). Categories cs.LG, cs.CL. The ar5iv text matches v2 content, but ar5iv does not label its version.
  - The abs page shows no journal-ref or venue. The QuIP# paper's own bibliography cites QuIP as "Thirty-seventh Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems, 2023" (NeurIPS 2023). That is a citation in another paper, not a statement on the QuIP arXiv page.
  
  2) QuIP# (Tseng, Chee, Sun, Kuleshov, De Sa), arXiv 2402.04396v2
  
  a. BlockLDLQ
  - Block LDL: H = L^T D L, with L unit block lower triangular and D block diagonal, with block size g (Sec. 4.1). U = L^T − I. Per the text, the update is Ŵ_k = Q(W_k + (W_{:(k−1)} − Ŵ_{:(k−1)}) A_k), where A_k holds the k−g+1 through k columns of U. Ŵ = Q(W + (W − Ŵ)(L^T − I)) is the matrix form stated in Sec. 4.1.
  - Theorem 4.1 (Sec. 4.1), quoted: "Suppose that HH is μ-incoherent, and that we use a (possibly stochastic) vector quantizer Q that satisfies E[(Q(x)−x)(Q(x)−x)^T] ⪯ σ²I for any x ∈ R^g."
  Bound: E[tr((Ŵ−W)H(Ŵ−W)^T)] ≤ (g m μ² σ²/ n) tr(H^{1/2})².
  The text says the naive block-independent bound is g m σ² tr(H), and that the improvement is "the same factor achieved in the scalar case in QuIP."
  - Dependence: g is the block size, n the Hessian dimension, μ the incoherence, and the bound depends on tr(H^{1/2})². (The text calls the block size d in places, and the model-summary of the rest of the paper calls it d; the theorem uses g.)
  - Lemma B.1 (μ²/n tr(H^{1/2})² bound on tr(D)) is in Appendix B, which I did not read verbatim. Not verified.
  
  b. E8 and E8P (Sec. 4 intro, Sec. 4.2, Sec. 6.3)
  - Sec. 4 intro: "E8 lattice, which achieves the highest density unit ball packing in R^8". Sec. 4.2 notes the E8 "codebooks achieve lower MSEs than all other presented codebooks ... due to the underlying packing density and high dimensionality of E8" (Figure 3 caption).
  - Sec. 4.2 E8P: "2-bit 8 dimensional E8P codebook, which contains 2^16 entries but only requires lookups into a 2^8-entry table, with the remaining 8 bits being used to store signs and shifts." Also: "E8P requires only 1KiB of space" (Sec. 4.2 and Sec. 6.3).
  - Sec. 4.2 construction: S = 227 elements of |D̂8| with norm ≤ √10 plus 29 padding elements of norm √12. The 16-bit codeword is 8 bits (entry of S), 7 sign-flip bits, and 1 bit for ±1/4.
  - Sec. 4.3: 2 bits is E8P. 4 bits is residual VQ with E8P applied twice. 3 bits is E8P plus a 1-bit E8 codebook. Read from the page text, Sec. 4.3 verbatim.
  - Bits overhead: Sec. 5 and Appendix F.1 (the latter from the model summary, not verbatim) say sign vectors add less than 0.01 bits per weight.
  
  c. Shape and Gaussianity
  - Sec. 4 intro (verbatim): "It follows from the central

  1) Agrell & Allen, "On the best lattice quantizers" (arXiv:2202.09605)
  Read: https://arxiv.org/abs/2202.09605 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2202.09605v2 (truncated at 39,956 of 97,738 chars; rest is summary) ; https://arxiv.org/pdf/2202.09605 (returned binary, no readable text, so I used the v2 HTML instead; ar5iv not needed).
  a. NSM values:
  - E8: 0.071682099, Table I ("The best known lattice quantizers"), "Best previously reported" row, n=8 (summary). Matches 929/12960 ≈ 0.0716821.
  - Z^n: 0.083333333 = 1/12. Table I's entry is the n=1 row; Corollary 8 proof states "G₂ = G(Q̂_ℤ) = 1/12" (summary).

  c. Venue/version: IEEE Trans. Information Theory, vol. 69, no. 12, pp. 7650–7658, Dec. 2023, DOI 10.1109/TIT.2023.3291313 (arXiv abs page). arXiv v1 19 Feb 2022; v2 28 Jun 2023 (the HTML version). Primary class cs.IT.
  
  2) Tseng, Sun, De Sa, "Model-Preserving Adaptive Rounding" (YAQA), arXiv:2505.22988
  Read: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.22988 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2505.22988v3 (truncated at 39,956 of 114,139 chars; rest is summary).
  a. Quotes:
  - Abstract: "most quantization algorithms minimize the immediate activation error of each layer as a proxy for the end-to-end error." Same abstract continues: "this ignores the effect of future layers, making it a poor proxy."
  - Sec. 2.1.1: "In GPTQ and LDLQ, which are equivalent, columns in W∗ are iteratively rounded with linear feedback..."
  - Sec. 1 (intro): GPTQ "rounds using the Hessian of the immediate layerwise activation error as a proxy". The paper does not literally say GPTQ/LDLQ ignore KL in one sentence; the KL argument is in the abstract, Sec. 1, and Eq. (1).
  b. Theory:
  - Eq. (1): θ̂ ← argmin_{θ∈C} E_{X∼D}[D_KL(M(θ*,X) ‖ M(θ,X))].
  - Eq. (2): ℒ(W) ≈ ½ vec(W−W*)ᵀ (∇²ℒ(W*)) vec(W−W*). First-order terms vanish since D_KL is minimized at the original model.
  - Eq. (3), LDLQ: W = 𝒬(W* + (W*−W)(L₁−I)). LDLQ minimizes tr((W*−W)H₁(W*−W)ᵀ), H₁ = E[xᵀx] (Sec. 2.1.1).
  - Eq. (4)/(5): YAQA with H̃ = H_O ⊗ H_I; Eq. (5): W = vec⁻¹(𝒬(vec(W*) + vec(Δ)(L_O⊗L_I − I))), Δ = W*−W. Eq. (6) is expanded form.
  - Theorem 3.4: bounds vec(Δ)H vec(Δ)ᵀ via cosine similarity c = ⟨H,H_O⊗H_I⟩/(‖H‖_F‖H_O‖_F‖H_I‖_F).
  - Sketch power iteration: Eqs. (8)–(9) generic; Sketch A Eqs. (10)–(11); Sketch B Eqs. (12)–(13). Kronecker-factored: H ≈ H_O ⊗ H_I (Sec. 2.2, Sec. 3.2).
  c. ≈30% claim: HTML v3 abstract: "empirically reduces the KL divergence by ≈30% over these methods." Intro: "Empirically, YAQA reduces the KL by ≈30% over LDLQ." NOTE: the arXiv abs page abstract for the same v3 instead says "reduces the error by ≈30% over these methods". (summary) Table 1 caption "YAQA reduces the KL by 30% over LDLQ" and Sec. 4.3 "≈10-20% over LDLQ" (finetuning) are in the unread part.
  d. Version/venue: arXiv v3, revised 3 Jun 2026 (v1 29 May 2025; v2 26 Sep 2025). arXiv comments: "ICML 2026". Acceptance not otherwise verified.
  
  3) Yang, Kautz, Hatamizadeh, "Gated Delta Networks: Improving Mamba2 with Delta Rule" (arXiv:2412.06464)
  Read: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.06464 ; https://arxiv.org/html/2412.06464 (HTML v3, truncated at 39,958 of 117,992 chars; rest is summary).
  a. Equations:
  - Eq. (10), Sec. 3.1, gated delta rule (state update): S_t = S_{t−1}(α_t(I − β_t k_t k_tᵀ)) + β_t v_t k_tᵀ, α_t ∈ (0,1), β_t ∈ (0,1). (The summary wrongly said Eq. (10) was missing; it is in the verbatim text.)
  - Table 1, row "Gated DeltaNet": same update, S_t = S_{t−1}(α_t(I − β_t k_t k_tᵀ)) + β_t v_t k_tᵀ.
  - Output: no explicit per-token gated output equation is in readable text. Sec. 2.1 gives the base recurrence output o_t = S_t q_t (unnumbered). Gated chunkwise output O_[t] in Sec. 3.3 is unnumbered. So the output equation is implicit; I did not find a numbered one.
  b. Version/venue: arXiv v3, revised 6 Mar 2025 (v1 9 Dec 2024; v2 5 Mar 2025). arXiv comments: "ICLR 2025 camera ready."

# Where does the damage come from?  Quantize ONE family of matrices at a time (rest BF16), 3 scale rules,
# no-feedback E8 at the control multiplier (same sigma/step for every rule via each rule's own calibrated mult).
import json, time
st = H.S["st"]; mats = H.qmats(); docs = list(range(0, 72, 3))
groups = {"mlp_in(gate,up)": ("gate_proj", "up_proj"), "mlp_down": ("down_proj",), "gdn_in(qkv,z,a,b)": ("in_proj_qkv", "in_proj_z", "in_proj_a", "in_proj_b"),
          "gdn_out": ("out_proj",), "attn_in(q,k,v)": ("q_proj", "k_proj", "v_proj"), "attn_out": ("o_proj",), "embed": ("embed",), "lm_head": ("lm_head",)}
mults = {"lumen": 0.5917, "col": 0.5722, "fisher_local": 0.5066}
pname = lambda m: "lm_head.weight" if m["kind"] == "lm_head" else "model.language_model." + m["name"] + ".weight"
H.restore(); res = {}
for g, kinds in groups.items():
    sub = [m for m in mats if m["kind"] in kinds]; names = [pname(m) for m in sub]
    for rule, mult in mults.items():
        if g == "embed" and rule != "lumen": continue
        if g == "lm_head" and rule == "fisher_local": continue
        b = 0; n = 0
        for m in sub:
            bb, nn = H.quantize_matrix(m, dict(rule=rule), mult, st); b += bb; n += nn
        r = H.evaluate("select", docs=docs)
        res[f"{g}|{rule}"] = dict(kl=r["kl"], agree=r["agree"], bpw=b/n, params=n)
        print("%-20s %-13s bpw=%.3f params=%.2fB KL=%.5f agree=%.4f" % (g, rule, b/n, n/1e9, r["kl"], r["agree"]), flush=True)
        H.restore(names)
json.dump(res, open(H.OUT+"/diag_bykind_rules.json", "w"))

import time, json, math
st = H.S["st"]; mats = H.qmats()
N = sum(m["mod"].weight.numel() for m in mats)
TARGET_BPW = 2.824          # Lumen "Max" symbol capacity 2.8328 minus measured codec overhead 0.009
target = TARGET_BPW * N
H.S["target_bits"] = target; H.S["N_text"] = N
def run(vid, spec, mats=mats, lo=0.25, hi=2.0):
    t=time.time()
    H.restore()
    mult = H.calibrate_mult(mats, spec, target, st, lo=lo, hi=hi, iters=11)
    info = H.quantize_all(mats, spec, mult, st)
    info.update(spec=spec, mult=mult, target_bits=target, bits_over_target=info["bits"]/target-1, bytes=info["bits"]/8)
    print(vid, "mult=%.4f bpw=%.4f bits/target-1=%+.4f%%" % (mult, info["bpw"], 100*info["bits_over_target"]), {k: round(v,3) for k,v in info["bykind"].items()}, "quant %.0fs"%(time.time()-t), flush=True)
    return H.run_variant(vid, info), mult
r0, m0 = run("A0_Q0rtn", dict(rule="lumen"))
H.S["mult_Q0rtn"] = m0
run("A0_Qcol_rtn", dict(rule="col"))
run("A0_Qfl_rtn", dict(rule="fisher_local"))
run("A0_Qfg_rtn", dict(rule="fisher_global", io_mult=m0), lo=1e-6, hi=1e-1)

{"command": "cd /tmp/claude-0/-workspace/78f73527-ae7c-4365-8749-6095616e0753/scratchpad && tail -2 colenergy.log && cat > retro.py <<'EOF'\n# Retrodiction of the recorded per-family RTN tests (diag_bykind_rules.json) with the F1 noise model.\n# RTN proxy error of a matrix with column scales c:  (G mult^2 / n) * (sum_j C_j c_j^2) * (sum_j X_j / c_j^2)\nimport torch, json, math, collections\nce=torch.load('colenergy.pt'); st=torch.load('/workspace/work/stats_fisher.pt', map_location='cpu'); X=st['X']\nmeas=json.load(open('/workspace/LUMEN_Q1_V3.1/formic_arch/results/diag_bykind_rules.json'))\ngroups={\"mlp_in(gate,up)\":(\"gate_proj\",\"up_proj\"),\"mlp_down\":(\"down_proj\",),\"gdn_in(qkv,z,a,b)\":(\"in_proj_qkv\",\"in_proj_z\",\"in_proj_a\",\"in_proj_b\"),\n        \"gdn_out\":(\"out_proj\",),\"attn_in(q,k,v)\":(\"q_proj\",\"k_proj\",\"v_proj\"),\"attn_out\":(\"o_proj\",),\"lm_head\":(\"lm_head\",)}\nml,mc=0.5917,0.5722\ndef fam(kinds):\n    return [k for k in X if k.split('.')[-1] in kinds]\nprint(\"family | measured KL ratio col/lumen | model proxy ratio (alpha=1 incl. mult) : sum-weighted, median | half-power gain gamma (theta=1) : sum-weighted, median\")\nfor g,kinds in groups.items():\n    num=0.0; den=0.0; rs=[]; gs=[]; gnum=0.0\n    for k in fam(kinds):\n        C=ce[k]['col'].double(); x=X[k].double().clamp_min(float(X[k].double().mean())*1e-6); n=len(x)\n        nat=float(C.sum()*x.sum())                      # native proxy (up to G mult^2/n)\n        a1=float((C*x).sum())*n                         # alpha=1 : (sum C x / GM)(sum x / (x/GM)) = n * sum C x\n        r=(mc/ml)**2*a1/nat\n        gam=float((C*x).sqrt().sum()**2)/nat            # Cauchy-Schwarz optimum (half-power gauge)\n        num+=(mc/ml)**2*a1; den+=nat; gnum+=gam*nat; rs.append(r); gs.append(gam)\n    rs.sort(); gs.sort()\n    m=meas[g+'|col']['kl']/meas[g+'|lumen']['kl']\n    print(f\"{g:20s} | {m:5.3f} | {num/den:6.3f}  {rs[len(rs)//2]:6.3f} | {gnum/den:5.3f}  {gs[len(gs)//2]:5.3f}\")\nEOF\nCUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=\"\" timeout 100 python3 -I retro.py", "description": "Retrodict recorded per-family tests with the noise model using column energies"}

{"command": "cd /tmp/claude-0/-workspace/78f73527-ae7c-4365-8749-6095616e0753/scratchpad && cat > beta.py <<'EOF'\n# Calibrate, per family, the exponent beta of the column KL-weight  omega_j ~ X_j^beta  from the recorded alpha=1 RTN family test,\n# then predict the best exponent alpha* and the corresponding gain.  Family KL model (RTN, diagonal):\n#   K(alpha) ~ sum_matrices (G mult^2/n) (sum_j C_j X_j^alpha) (sum_j X_j^(beta-alpha))\nimport torch, json, math\nce=torch.load('colenergy.pt'); st=torch.load('/workspace/work/stats_fisher.pt', map_location='cpu'); X=st['X']\nmeas=json.load(open('/workspace/LUMEN_Q1_V3.1/formic_arch/results/diag_bykind_rules.json'))\ngroups={\"mlp_in\":(\"gate_proj\",\"up_proj\"),\"mlp_down\":(\"down_proj\",),\"gdn_in\":(\"in_proj_qkv\",\"in_proj_z\",\"in_proj_a\",\"in_proj_b\"),\n        \"gdn_out\":(\"out_proj\",),\"attn_in\":(\"q_proj\",\"k_proj\",\"v_proj\"),\"attn_out\":(\"o_proj\",),\"lm_head\":(\"lm_head\",)}\nmkey={\"mlp_in\":\"mlp_in(gate,up)\",\"mlp_down\":\"mlp_down\",\"gdn_in\":\"gdn_in(qkv,z,a,b)\",\"gdn_out\":\"gdn_out\",\"attn_in\":\"attn_in(q,k,v)\",\"attn_out\":\"attn_out\",\"lm_head\":\"lm_head\"}\nml,mc=0.5917,0.5722\ndef K(ks,alpha,beta):\n    tot=0.0\n    for k in ks:\n        C=ce[k]['col'].double(); x=X[k].double(); x=x.clamp_min(float(x.mean())*1e-6); lx=x.log()\n        lx=lx-lx.mean()                                   # geometric-mean normalisation (scale-free)\n        tot+=float((C*(alpha*lx).exp()).sum()*(((beta-alpha)*lx).exp()).sum())/len(x)*float(x.log().mean().exp())**beta\n    return tot\nprint(\"family     measured  beta_fit | predicted K(alpha)/K(0) at alpha=0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1 (same mult) | alpha* , K(alpha*)/K(0)\")\nfor g,kinds in groups.items():\n    ks=[k for k in X if k.split('.')[-1] in kinds]\n    m=meas[mkey[g]+'|col']['kl']/meas[mkey[g]+'|lumen']['kl']/(mc/ml)**2      # ratio at equal multiplier\n    f=lambda b: K(ks,1.0,b)/K(ks,0.0,b)-m\n    lo,hi=-3.0,3.0\n    if f(lo)*f(hi)>0:\n        print(\"%-9s %.3f  no root in [-3,3]: f(-3)=%.3f f(3)=%.3f\"%(g,m,f(lo)+m,f(hi)+m)); continue\n    for _ in range(60):\n        mid=(lo+hi)/2\n        if f(lo)*f(mid)<=0: hi=mid\n        else: lo=mid\n    b=(lo+hi)/2; k0=K(ks,0.0,b)\n    grid=[i/40-1 for i in range(0,101)]; vals=[K(ks,a,b)/k0 for a in grid]; ia=min(range(len(grid)),key=lambda i:vals[i])\n    print(\"%-9s %.3f    %+.2f   | %.3f %.3f %.3f %.3f | %+.2f  %.3f\"%(g,m,b,K(ks,.25,b)/k0,K(ks,.5,b)/k0,K(ks,.75,b)/k0,K(ks,1.0,b)/k0,grid[ia],vals[ia]))\nEOF\nCUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES=\"\" timeout 110 python3 -I beta.py", "description": "Fit per-family KL-weight exponent from recorded tests and predict optimal gauge exponent"}
